Convexity in Disguise: A Theoretical Framework for Nonconvex Low-Rank Matrix Estimation
Chengyu Cui, Gongjun Xu

Abstract
Nonconvex methods have emerged as a dominant approach for low-rank matrix estimation, a problem that arises widely in machine learning and AI for learning and representing high-dimensional data. Existing analyses for these methods often require additional regularization to mitigate nonconvexity, even though such regularization is often unnecessary in practice. Moreover, most analyses rely on problem-specific arguments that are difficult to generalize to more complex settings. In this paper, we develop a theoretical framework for studying nonconvex procedures across a broad class of low-rank matrix estimation problems. Rather than focusing on a specific model, we reveal a fundamental mechanism that explains why nonconvex procedures can behave well in low-rank estimation. Our key device is a benign regularizer that does not alter the original update rule, but yields an equivalent locally strongly convex formulation of the algorithm. This perspective uncovers a disguised convexity inherent in the nonconvex procedure and provides a new route to theoretical guarantees for nonconvex low-rank matrix estimation.



1
Introduction

Low-rank matrix estimation arises in a wide range of problems in statistics, machine learning, and information processing
[
9
,
17
,
20
]
.
Recently, low-rank structure has also become a central tool in modern machine learning, such as parameter-efficient adaptation of large models
[
25
,
52
,
53
]
, efficient Transformer attention via low-rank approximations
[
49
,
51
]
, memory-efficient training and model compression
[
50
,
54
]
, and implicit low-rank regularization in deep learning
[
1
]
.
The growing use of low-rank structure in increasingly complex models motivates a theoretical framework to understand the optimization geometry underlying low-rank estimation.

In this paper, we study the problem of estimating a rank-
r
matrix
\bm{X}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
by minimizing a general loss function
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
r
is typically much smaller than
\min\{n,q\}
. Direct optimization of
\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})
subject to the rank constraint on
\bm{X}
is often computationally challenging, which is NP-hard in several important settings
[
39
]
. A common approach is to parameterize
\bm{X}
as
\bm{X}=\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal}
, where
\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
and
\bm{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times r}
, often referred to as the Burer–Monteiro factorization
[
4
]
. Then the estimation problem can be reformulated as the following nonconvex problem:

(\widehat{\bm{U}},\widehat{\bm{V}})\;=\;{\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}\times\mathbb{R}^{q\times r}}\,\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal}).

(1)

A common approach to solving this problem is gradient descent, which updates
(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})
at step
t
by

\bm{U}^{t+1}=\bm{U}^{t}-q^{-1}\eta^{t}\nabla_{\bm{U}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}^{t}(\bm{V}^{t})^{\intercal}),\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{V}^{t+1}=\bm{V}^{t}-n^{-1}\eta^{t}\nabla_{\bm{V}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}^{t}(\bm{V}^{t})^{\intercal}),

(2)

where
\eta^{t}
is the learning rate,
\nabla_{\bm{U}}\mathcal{L}
is the gradient of
\mathcal{L}
w.r.t.
\bm{U}
, and
n^{-1},q^{-1}
are scaling factors.

The primary challenge in analyzing (
2
) is the nonconvexity of
\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal})
. In particular, regardless of the specific form or structural properties of
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
, one fundamental difficulty that leads to nonconvexity remains: the factorization is non-identifiable, since
(\bm{U},\bm{V})
and
(\bm{U}\bm{G},\bm{V}\bm{G}^{-{\intercal}})
yield identical loss for any invertible
\bm{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}

[
19
]
. To address this issue, many existing works introduce additional regularization or constraints to (
2
). Popular strategies include truncating selected measurements or updates to control outliers or incoherence
[
16
,
27
,
32
]
; adding penalty terms
[
12
,
41
,
45
,
55
,
48
]
; and projecting the iterates onto certain sets based on prior knowledge
[
13
,
55
,
35
]
.
While these modifications provide convergence guarantees in various models, they are often unnecessary in practice and introduce additional tuning parameters. More importantly, much of the existing analysis relies crucially on these regularization mechanisms, which makes it difficult to understand the geometry of the problem and generalize to complex settings.

Recently, a growing body of work has focused on the original regularization-free scheme (
2
), motivated not only by its strong empirical performance despite the nonconvexity of
\mathcal{L}
, but also by its potential to shed light on the underlying geometry of the problem
[
1
,
22
,
31
,
33
,
43
]
.
For example,
33
showed that, in noiseless low-rank matrix sensing and under suitable initialization, gradient descent converges linearly without an explicit balancing penalty
\|\bm{U}^{\intercal}\bm{U}-\bm{V}^{\intercal}\bm{V}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
.
Similarly,
31
studied unregularized low-rank matrix recovery and established benign global landscape properties in the exact recovery setting.
However, these analyses rely heavily on problem-specific structures and are therefore difficult to extend. Specifically, they are largely limited to noiseless settings and often rely on the squared loss, whose gradient has a closed-form expression that can be decomposed into simpler components and analyzed separately.
Moreover, the resulting algebraic derivations may obscure the geometry of the problem, limiting insight into the underlying mechanism.

In this paper, we develop a theoretical framework for the nonconvex procedure (
2
) under a broad class of loss functions
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
. Our analysis offers new insight into the underlying geometry and provides a new route to establishing convergence guarantees. Our contributions are summarized as follows.

1.

We develop a novel framework for analyzing nonconvex
procedures in low-rank matrix estimation. Our key finding is that (
2
) can be mapped to mirror a gradient update on a locally strongly convex objective. This is achieved via a
benign regularizer
that leaves the original update unchanged while augmenting the objective to be locally strongly convex, under which error contraction can be established. Remarkably, because the update itself is not changed, this mirrored convex formulation suggests that the nonconvex procedure already contains an implicit convex structure, with our benign regularizer acting to reveal this structural feature. Our analysis provides a new way to understand the geometry underlying the problem.

2.

We establish convergence guarantees for the nonconvex procedure over a general class of loss functions under localized regularity conditions. A key feature of our theory is that these regularity conditions are imposed only on small neighborhoods around the true parameter, rather than globally, thereby enabling convergence analysis for general loss functions. This localization makes the conditions applicable across a broad range of problems, but also necessitates delicate control of the entire trajectory.
Our convex formulation offers a new route to establish iterative contraction bounds along the full algorithmic path under suitable initialization, thereby ensuring that the iterates remain within the local region throughout the analysis.

3.

We establish convergence guarantees for estimation under statistical noise. Quantifying how noise reshapes the optimization landscape of regularization-free nonconvex procedures remains a major challenge. Under our general loss framework, we identify key quantities that characterize how statistical noise perturbs the estimation problem, and prove linear convergence guarantees in its presence. We demonstrate the applicability of our theory through two representative examples for which such convergence guarantees were previously unavailable.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
introduces our framework. Section
3
describes how the framework handles statistical noise. Section
4
concludes. In the appendices, we provide additional technical details and proofs of all theoretical results.

Notation.
For any integer
n
, let
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
. For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, let
a\vee b=\max(a,b)
and
a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
. For matrix
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
, we denote by
\|\bm{M}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
,
\|\bm{M}\|
, and
\|\bm{M}\|_{2\to\infty}
the Frobenius norm, the operator norm, and the two-to-infinity norm, respectively.
The
k
th largest singular value of a matrix
\bm{M}
is denoted by
\sigma_{k}(\bm{M})
. For a symmetric matrix
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, denote by
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{M})
its minimal eigenvalue. We write
\bm{I}_{n}
for the
n\times n
identity matrix and
{\bm{0}}
for zero vector or zero matrix when the dimension is clear from the context. For any positive integer
r
, define
\mathcal{O}^{r}=\{\bm{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}:\bm{R}\bm{R}^{\intercal}=\bm{I}_{r}\}
and
GL(r)=\{\bm{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}:\bm{G}\text{ is invertible}\}
.

2
Optimization without Noise

2.1
A Warm-up under Special Symmetric Model

We start with a special symmetric model in which
n=q
and
\bm{X}
is symmetric, namely,
\bm{U}=\bm{V}
. This setting exhibits nonconvexity similar to that of the asymmetric model
[
34
]
. Thus, we use it as the starting point for introducing our main ideas. At the same time, the technical development still differs in meaningful ways from that of the asymmetric model, so the symmetric problem is also of independent interest.
To avoid ambiguity, we use
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
to denote the factorization replacing
\bm{U}=\bm{V}
. In this section, we consider the noiseless case where the true parameter is a stationary point of
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
, i.e.,
\nabla_{\bm{X}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}^{*}(\bm{Z}^{*})^{\intercal})={\bm{0}}
, with the noisy setting presented in Section
3
. The gradient update is then given as

\bm{Z}^{t+1}\;=\;\bm{Z}^{t}-n^{-1}\eta^{t}\nabla_{\bm{Z}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{Z}^{t}{}^{\intercal})\;=\;\bm{Z}^{t}-2n^{-1}\eta^{t}\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}^{t})\bm{Z}^{t},

(3)

where
\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}):=\nabla_{\bm{X}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})
and the second equality follows by chain rule.
Under the symmetric model, the factorization is identifiable only up to orthogonal transformation: for any
\bm{R}\in\mathcal{O}^{r}
,
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{Z}\bm{R}
yield the same loss
\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})
. To measure error relative to the true factorization
\bm{Z}^{*}
, we adopt the standard alignment argument where for any
\bm{Z}
, we let

\bm{R}^{*}=\;{\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}}_{\bm{R}\in\mathcal{O}^{r}}\|\bm{Z}\bm{R}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}},

(4)

and define the
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
distances between
\bm{Z}
and
\bm{Z}^{*}
by

dist_{2}(\bm{Z},\bm{Z}^{*}):=\|\bm{Z}\bm{R}^{*}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}},\qquad dist_{\infty}(\bm{Z},\bm{Z}^{*}):=\|\bm{Z}\bm{R}^{*}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}.

Equation (
4
) is often referred to as Wahba’s problem
[
47
]
, or the orthogonal Procrustes problem
[
40
]
.

Establishing contraction for (
3
) is very challenging. Standard convex optimization theory does not apply, because one always has
\lambda_{\min}\big[\nabla_{\bm{z}}^{2}\{\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})\}|_{\bm{Z}=\bm{Z}^{*}}\big]=0
for
\bm{z}=:\mathrm{vec}(\bm{Z}^{\intercal})
. This reflects the intrinsic nonconvexity caused by the nonlinear map
\bm{Z}\mapsto\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal}
. To address this, prior
works typically introduce penalties
[
27
,
41
]
or use additional projection steps
[
13
,
35
]
. A different approach is taken by
34
for matrix completion, who constructs a region with restricted directions in which the bilinear Hessian admits a suitable lower bound, and then shows that the iterates remain in this region, with the estimation error lying along these directions throughout the updates. This approach is problem-specific and difficult to extend beyond linear models.

We now introduce our method.
Our idea is to introduce a benign regularizer term whose gradient vanishes across the iterates
\{\bm{Z}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, yet the augmented objective becomes (strongly) convex. At first sight, such a benign regularizer seems impossible to construct, since this favorable property must hold uniformly along the entire trajectory of the iterates. The key observation comes from the error metric. To measure the error of each iterate
\bm{Z}^{t}
, one needs the optimal alignment
\bm{R}_{t}^{*}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{R}\in\mathcal{O}^{r}}\|\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
. By Theorem 2 in
42
,
\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}
always satisfies that
(\bm{Z}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}^{*}_{t}=(\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}^{*}_{t})^{\intercal}\bm{Z}^{*}
is positive semidefinite. Motivated by this property, we propose the following benign regularizer for the symmetric model:

p^{*}_{\lambda}(\bm{Z})\;=\;\tfrac{\lambda n^{2}}{4}\|n^{-1}(\bm{Z}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{Z}-n^{-1}\bm{Z}^{\intercal}\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},\quad\lambda>0

where
\lambda n^{2}/4
is a scaling factor with
\lambda
specified later. The regularizer has two key properties

(i)

p^{*}_{\lambda}(\bm{Z})
is benign for the gradient update:
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}\,p_{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}^{*}_{t})={\bm{0}},

\forall t\in\mathbb{N},\lambda>0
. Therefore, after multiplying (
3
) on the right by
\bm{R}_{t}^{*}
, the gradient update can be equivalently written as

\bm{Z}^{t+1}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}=\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}-\Big\{2\frac{\eta^{t}}{n}\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}^{t})\bm{Z}^{t}\Big\}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}=\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}-\frac{\eta^{t}}{n}\Big\{2\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*})\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}+\nabla_{\bm{Z}}p^{*}_{\lambda}(\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*})\Big\},

(5)

where the last equality uses
\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*})=\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}^{t})
, which holds by definition.

(ii)

h_{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{Z}):=\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})+p_{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{Z})
is strongly convex within a local region around
\bm{Z}^{*}
for suitable
\lambda
(See Lemma
1
below). Consequently, (
5
) yields

dist_{2}(\bm{Z}^{t+1},\bm{Z}^{*})\;\leq\;\|\bm{Z}^{t+1}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\;=\;\|\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}-\tfrac{\eta^{t}}{n}\nabla_{\bm{Z}}h_{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*})-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

(6)

This implies that the original update
\bm{Z}^{t}\mapsto\bm{Z}^{t+1}
admits the equivalent form
\bm{Z}^{t}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}\mapsto\bm{Z}^{t+1}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}
, which contracts under gradient descent on the strongly convex objective
h_{\lambda}^{*}(\cdot)
with the next-step error dominating
dist_{2}(\bm{Z}^{t+1},\bm{Z}^{*})
. In other words, the original iterates evolve like the gradient descent on a locally strongly convex objective in the sense of error contraction.

While it may seem surprising that adding
p_{\lambda}^{*}(\cdot)
yields a strongly convex landscape, we emphasize that this is not an artificial structure. Rather, the formulation reveals an implicit structural feature already present in the original update. Specifically,
p_{\lambda}^{*}(\cdot)
is constructed from the first-order condition for (
4
), which is the standard error metric under this setup. Our innovation is to use optimal alignment not merely for post-hoc error measurement, but to construct a regularizer that benignly augments the loss. Moreover, it should not be concerning that the aligned update (
5
) involves the true parameter
\bm{Z}^{*}
, since this update is introduced only as an analytical device rather than an implemented algorithm. In fact,
\bm{Z}^{*}
already enters the analysis through
\bm{R}^{*}_{t}
used to define the
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
distances.

Now we show that
h_{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{Z})=\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})+p_{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{Z})
exhibits local strong convexity for any
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
with the local restricted isometry property (RIP). To this end, define the local regions of interest

\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)\!=\!\Big\{\bm{Z}:\frac{dist_{2}(\bm{Z},\bm{Z}^{*})}{\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}\leq\epsilon\Big\};\quad\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)\!=\!\Big\{\bm{Z}:\frac{dist_{2}(\bm{Z},\bm{Z}^{*})}{\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}\leq\epsilon\text{, }\frac{dist_{\infty}(\bm{Z},\bm{Z}^{*})}{\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}}\leq\epsilon\Big\}.

We allow the local radius
\epsilon
to vanish with the problem size. The following regularity conditions are required only within this possibly small local region, allowing our theory to accommodate complex settings where the global behavior may be unrestricted and complicated.

Assumption 1
.

There exist some
\epsilon
and
\beta\geq\alpha>0
such that, for either
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
, the following holds: for every
\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}
and every
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
,

\alpha\|\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\,\leq\,\nabla^{2}_{\bm{X}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})[\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W}),\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W})]\,\leq\,\beta\|\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

(7)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W}):=\bm{Z}\bm{W}^{\intercal}+\bm{W}\bm{Z}^{\intercal}
and
\nabla_{\bm{X}}^{2}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})[\cdot,\cdot]
denotes the Hessian bilinear form.

Assumption 2
.

There exist some
\epsilon
and a constant
L_{2}
such that for all
\bm{Z}_{1},\bm{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
,
\|\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}_{1})-\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}_{2})\|\leq L_{2}\|\bm{Z}_{1}\bm{Z}_{1}^{\intercal}-\bm{Z}_{2}\bm{Z}_{2}^{\intercal}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
, where
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
.

Remark 1
.

The restricted isometry property in Assumption
1
is a widely accepted condition in low-rank estimation problems
[
3
,
10
,
23
,
31
,
39
]
. Our condition is general enough to accommodate a broad range of settings, as (i) it is required only locally within the neighborhood
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
instead of a global one; (ii)
\beta
and
\alpha
are allowed to be of different orders. Such a local RIP can thus be easily satisfied in problems such as matrix sensing
[
33
,
38
,
45
]
and matrix completion
[
13
,
24
,
55
]
. Moreover, it is close to minimal for our analysis: without it, the original constrained problem
\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\mathrm{rank}(\bm{X})\leq r}\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})
may fail to be convex even in a neighborhood of the true parameter
\bm{X}^{*}
, representing an obstruction inherent to the loss function rather than the factorization.

Furthermore, Assumption
2
imposes a local Lipschitz condition on
\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z})
. This is a mild regularity requirement, ensuring that
\|\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z})\|
remains small and well controlled within a neighborhood of
\bm{Z}^{*}
.

The following result demonstrates the local strong convexity of
h_{\lambda}^{*}(\cdot)
under Assumptions
1
and
2
.

Lemma 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, and with
\lambda=\alpha
, for any
\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
and
\bm{R}^{*}
from (
4
),

\lambda_{\min}\big\{n^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{z}}^{2}h_{\alpha}^{*}(\bm{Z}\bm{R}^{*})\big\}\;\geq\;\alpha\sigma_{r}(n^{-1/2}\bm{Z}^{*})^{2}\,-\,4n^{-1}(\epsilon+2L_{2})\epsilon\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

For simplicity, we write
h_{\alpha}^{*}(\cdot)
and
p_{\alpha}^{*}(\cdot)
throughout the rest of the paper unless otherwise specified.

Local region
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
is smaller compared with
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
. When Assumptions
1
and
2
hold only for
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
, we need to show that the iterates remain inside
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
, which requires additional regularity conditions. To streamline the presentation and due to space limit, we defer these conditions, Assumptions
B.5
and
B.6
, and their discussion to Appendix
B
. We now state our result.

Theorem 1

(
Symmetric and Noiseless).

Let
\bm{X}^{*}=\bm{Z}^{*}(\bm{Z}^{*})^{\intercal}
,
\sigma_{\min}=\sigma_{r}(\bm{X}^{*})/n
, and
\kappa=\sigma_{1}(\bm{X}^{*})/\sigma_{r}(\bm{X}^{*})
. Let the iterates
\{\bm{Z}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be generated by (
3
) with step size
\eta^{t}=\eta=\{10(\alpha+\beta)\kappa\sigma_{\min}\}^{-1}
and
\rho:=1-\eta\alpha\sigma_{\min}/4
.

\ell_{2}
-error contraction.
Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
. Assume that the initialization
\bm{Z}^{0}
satisfies

{dist_{2}(\bm{Z}^{0},\bm{Z}^{*})}{}\leq\phi_{n}\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\quad\text{ with }\quad\phi_{n}\leq\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\wedge c_{0}\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa\sqrt{r}}

(8)

for some sequence
\phi_{n}
and some sufficiently small constant
c_{0}>0
. Then for all
t\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,

dist_{2}(\bm{Z}^{t},\bm{Z}^{*})\;\leq\;\rho^{t}\phi_{n}\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

(9)

\ell_{\infty}
-error contraction.
Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
,
B.5
, and
B.6
hold with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
. If
\bm{Z}^{0}
satisfies (
8
) and

{dist_{\infty}(\bm{Z}^{0},\bm{Z}^{*})}\leq\psi_{n}\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}\quad\text{ with }\quad\psi_{n}\leq\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\;\text{ and }\;\tfrac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{3/2}\sqrt{r}\tfrac{\phi_{n}}{\psi_{n}}\leq c_{0},

(10)

for some sequence
\psi_{n}
and sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
, then for all
t\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, (
9
) continues to hold, and in addition

dist_{\infty}(\bm{Z}^{t},\bm{Z}^{*})\;\leq\;\rho^{t}\psi_{n}\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}.

(11)

Theorem
1
establishes linear convergence of gradient descent under a general loss
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
, in both
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
errors. When
\alpha\asymp\beta
and
\kappa\asymp 1
, the step size satisfies
\eta\asymp 1
, and the iterates converge linearly at a constant rate
\rho
. Notably, the contraction gap
1-\rho
depends on
\alpha/\beta
and
\kappa^{-1}
, reflecting the effects of the local curvature
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
and the conditioning of
\bm{X}^{*}
on the problem geometry, respectively.

We remark that
\phi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}
need not equal the exact initialization error, and may be chosen more conservatively, provided they satisfy the required scaling conditions. This perspective is consistent with the nonconvex low-rank literature, where convergence is typically established under an initialization condition
[
7
,
34
,
33
,
37
,
55
]
. Initialization methods are then obtained in a model-specific manner, for example via spectral method
[
7
,
37
,
34
]
, nuclear norm minimization
[
48
]
, and universal singular value thresholding
[
11
,
35
]
. Since initialization is problem-specific and has been studied extensively in the literature, our focus here is on the gradient descent scheme, provided that the initialization lies in the local basin.

2.2
Asymmetric Model

We now turn to the asymmetric model (
1
) with gradient update (
2
), where
n
is not necessarily equal to
q
and
\bm{U},\bm{V}
are allowed to differ. Motivated by the previous analysis, our goal is to construct a benign regularizer tied to the error metric, and to show that the gradient update (
2
) admits an equivalent reformulation as a strongly convex problem. Let the true parameters be
(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})
satisfying
\nabla_{\bm{X}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}^{*}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal})={\bm{0}}
. We begin with the identification issue. Note that for any invertible transformation
\bm{G}\in GL(r)
,
(\bm{U},\bm{V})
and
(\bm{U}\bm{G},\bm{V}\bm{G}^{-{\intercal}})
yield the same loss
\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal})
. We then let

\bm{G}^{*}\;=\;{\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}}_{\bm{G}\in{GL}(r)}\,n^{-1}\|\bm{U}\bm{G}-\bm{U}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\,+\,q^{-1}\|\bm{V}\bm{G}^{-{\intercal}}-\bm{V}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

whenever the minimizer exists, and define the distances between
(\bm{U},\bm{V})
and
(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})
by

\displaystyle dist_{2}\big\{(\bm{U},\bm{V}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\big\}:=\,

\displaystyle\,\big(n^{-1}\|\bm{U}\bm{G}^{*}-\bm{U}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+q^{-1}\|\bm{V}(\bm{G}^{*})^{-{\intercal}}-\bm{V}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\big)^{1/2},

\displaystyle dist_{\infty}\big\{(\bm{U},\bm{V}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\big\}:=\,

\displaystyle\,\max\big\{\|\bm{U}\bm{G}^{*}-\bm{U}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty},\|\bm{V}(\bm{G}^{*})^{-{\intercal}}-\bm{V}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}\big\}.

Here, we use
n^{-1}
and
q^{-1}
to scale the Frobenius norm to make the subsequent derivation simpler.
By Lemma
10
in Appendix
D.1
, whenever
\bm{G}^{*}
exists, it holds that
p_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\bm{U}\bm{G}^{*},\bm{V}(\bm{G}^{*})^{-{\intercal}})=0
where
p_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the benign regularizer under the asymmetric model, given as

p_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\bm{U},\bm{V}):=\tfrac{\alpha nq}{4}\|n^{-1}(\bm{U}-\bm{U}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{U}-q^{-1}\bm{V}^{\intercal}(\bm{V}-\bm{V}^{*})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},\;\alpha>0.

Analogous to the symmetric model,
\alpha
here is chosen to match the lower curvature bound in the asymmetric case, as specified later in Assumption
3
.
Following the same idea, we define
h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\bm{U},\bm{V}):=\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal})+p_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\bm{U},\bm{V})
and reformulate (
2
) as a gradient update on
h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\bm{U},\bm{V})
, which is shown to be strongly convex in a local region after proper scaling of
n,q
(see Lemma
8
in Appendix
D.1
).

For each
t\geq 0
, set
\bm{G}_{t}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{G}\in{GL}(r)}n^{-1}\|\bm{U}^{t}\bm{G}-\bm{U}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+q^{-1}\|\bm{V}^{t}\bm{G}^{-{\intercal}}-\bm{V}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
. For now, we assume
\bm{G}_{t}^{*}
exists; its existence will be established later in the proof. Let

\bm{\Lambda}_{t}^{*}=(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{G}_{t}^{*};\qquad\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t}=\bm{U}^{t}\bm{G}_{t}^{*};\qquad\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t}=\bm{V}^{t}(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{-{\intercal}}.

Denote
\mathcal{G}(\bm{U},\bm{V})=\nabla_{\bm{X}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
. Then obviously,
\mathcal{G}(\bm{U},\bm{V})=\mathcal{G}(\bm{U}\bm{G}_{t}^{*},\bm{V}(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{-{\intercal}})
.
By the chain rule, one then has

\nabla_{\bm{U}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})\,=\,\nabla_{\bm{U}}\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t},\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t})(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{\intercal}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nabla_{\bm{V}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})\,=\,\nabla_{\bm{V}}\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t},\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t})(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{-{\intercal}}.

Moreover, Lemma
10
gives
\nabla_{\bm{U}}p_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t},\!\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t})\!=\!{\bm{0}}
and
\nabla_{\bm{V}}p_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t},\!\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t})\!=\!{\bm{0}}
. Thus by multiplying by
\bm{G}_{t}^{*}
and
(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{-{\intercal}}
and adding the corresponding gradients of
p_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\cdot)
, the update (
2
) can be written as

\displaystyle\bm{U}^{t+1}\bm{G}_{t}^{*}=

\displaystyle\bm{U}^{t}\bm{G}_{t}^{*}-\frac{\eta^{t}}{q}\nabla_{\bm{U}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})\bm{G}_{t}^{*}

(12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t}-\frac{\eta^{t}}{q}\nabla_{\bm{U}}h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t},\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t})-\frac{\eta^{t}}{q}\nabla_{\bm{U}}h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t},\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t})(\bm{\Lambda}_{t}^{*}-\bm{I}_{r}),

\displaystyle\bm{V}^{t+1}(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{-{\intercal}}=

\displaystyle\bm{V}^{t}(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{-{\intercal}}-\frac{\eta^{t}}{n}\nabla_{\bm{V}}h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\bm{U}^{t}\bm{G}_{t}^{*},\bm{V}^{t}(\bm{G}_{t}^{*})^{-{\intercal}})(\bm{\Lambda}_{t}^{*})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t}-\frac{\eta^{t}}{n}\nabla_{\bm{V}}h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t},\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t})-\frac{\eta^{t}}{n}\nabla_{\bm{V}}h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\widetilde{\bm{U}}^{t},\widetilde{\bm{V}}^{t})\big\{(\bm{\Lambda}_{t}^{*})^{-1}-\bm{I}_{r}\big\}.

That is, after alignment, the update consists of a gradient step on the augmented objective
h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\cdot)
, together with an additional perturbation arising from the error
\bm{\Lambda}^{*}_{t}-\bm{I}_{r}
and
(\bm{\Lambda}^{*}_{t})^{-{\intercal}}-\bm{I}_{r}
. This perturbation is specific to the asymmetric model, because, unlike in the symmetric case, the alignment matrix
\bm{G}_{t}^{*}
need not be orthogonal. To address this, we impose the balancing condition

n^{-1}(\bm{U}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{U}^{*}=\,q^{-1}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{V}^{*}.

This entails no loss of generality, since if
\bm{X}^{*}
has singular value decomposition
\bm{U}^{\star}\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}(\bm{V}^{\star})^{\intercal}
, we may take
\bm{U}^{*}=\bm{U}^{\star}(\bm{\Sigma}^{\star})^{1/2}
and
\bm{V}^{*}=\bm{V}^{\star}(\bm{\Sigma}^{\star})^{1/2}
.
Theoretically, we show that, provided that the initialization
(\bm{U}^{0},\bm{V}^{0})
is suitably balanced, i.e.,
\bm{G}_{0}^{*}
is close to some orthogonal matrix, each
\bm{G}_{t}^{*}
is guaranteed to exist and remains close to the same orthogonal matrix throughout the iterations. As a result,
\bm{\Lambda}_{t}^{*}\approx\bm{I}_{r}
, so the asymmetric update (
12
) also closely matches a gradient step on the strongly convex objective
h_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\cdot)
. Similar to the symmetric model, in (
12
), the original update rule is preserved, and the regularizer
p_{\alpha}^{\natural}(\cdot)
acts simply as the device to make explicit the structural feature.

Now we introduce two local regions as the asymmetric versions of
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
and
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon):=

\displaystyle\,\left\{(\bm{U},\bm{V}):dist_{2}\big\{(\bm{U},\bm{V}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\big\}\leq\epsilon\,\tau_{*}\right\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon):=

\displaystyle\,\left\{(\bm{U},\bm{V}):dist_{2}\big\{(\bm{U},\bm{V}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\big\}\leq\epsilon\,\tau_{*},\;dist_{\infty}\big\{(\bm{U},\bm{V}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\big\}\leq\epsilon\,\omega_{*}\right\},

where
\tau_{*}{}^{2}:=({n^{-1}\|\bm{U}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+q^{-1}\|\bm{V}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}})/{2}
and
\omega_{*}:={\|\bm{U}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}}\vee{\|\bm{V}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}}
. The following are asymmetric analogues of Assumptions
1
and
2
, respectively.

Assumption 3
.

There exist
\epsilon
and
\beta\geq\alpha>0
such that, for either
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
, the following holds: for every
(\bm{U},\bm{V})\in\mathcal{D}
and every
(\bm{L},\bm{R})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}\times\mathbb{R}^{q\times r}
,

\alpha\|\bm{U}\bm{R}^{\intercal}+\bm{L}\bm{V}^{\intercal}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\nabla_{\bm{X}}^{2}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal})\big[\mathcal{P}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})}(\bm{R},\bm{L}),\mathcal{P}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})}(\bm{R},\bm{L})\big]\leq\beta{\|\bm{U}\bm{R}^{\intercal}+\bm{L}\bm{V}^{\intercal}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}},

where
\mathcal{P}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})}(\bm{R},\bm{L})=\bm{U}\bm{R}^{\intercal}+\bm{L}\bm{V}^{\intercal}
.

Assumption 4
.

There exist a constant
L_{2}
such that for all
(\bm{U}_{1},\bm{V}_{1}),(\bm{U}_{2},\bm{V}_{2})\in\mathcal{D}
,
\big\|\mathcal{G}(\bm{U}_{1},\bm{V}_{1})-\mathcal{G}(\bm{U}_{2},\bm{V}_{2})\big\|\leq L_{2}\big\|\bm{U}_{1}\bm{V}_{1}{}^{\intercal}-\bm{U}_{2}\bm{V}_{2}{}^{\intercal}\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}
where
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
.

To establish
\ell_{\infty}
error contraction, we require two additional technical conditions, Assumptions
B.7
and
B.8
, which are presented in Appendix
B
due to space limit. We now state the result.

Theorem 2

(
Asymmetric and Noiseless).

Let
\bm{X}^{*}=\bm{U}^{*}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal}
and
\kappa=\sigma_{1}(\bm{X}^{*})/\sigma_{r}(\bm{X}^{*})
. Assume that
(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})
are balanced:

n^{-1}(\bm{U}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{U}^{*}=\,q^{-1}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{V}^{*},\;\text{ with }\;\sigma_{\min}:={\sigma_{r}(\bm{X}^{*})}/{\sqrt{nq}}={\sigma_{r}(\bm{U}^{*})^{2}}/{n}={\sigma_{r}(\bm{V}^{*})^{2}}/{q}.

Let
\{\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be generated by (
2
) with
\eta^{t}=\eta=\{10(\alpha+\beta)\kappa\sigma_{\min}\}^{-1}
and
\rho:=1-\eta\alpha\sigma_{\min}/4
.

\ell_{2}
-error contraction.
Suppose Assumptions
3
–
4
hold with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
. Assume that there is some orthogonal matrix
\bm{R}^{0}
such that the initialization
(\bm{U}^{0},\bm{V}^{0})
satisfies

{\big(n^{-1}\|\bm{U}^{0}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{U}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+q^{-1}\|\bm{V}^{0}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{V}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\big)^{1/2}}\leq\;\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{nq}{\tau_{*}}\;\text{ with }\;\phi_{nq}\leq\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\wedge c_{0}\tfrac{\alpha(\alpha+\kappa)}{\beta\kappa^{2}\sqrt{r\kappa}},

(13)

for some sequence
\phi_{nq}
and some sufficiently small constant
c_{0}>0
.
Then for all
t\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we have

dist_{2}\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\}\;\leq\;\rho^{t}\phi_{nq}\tau_{*}.

(14)

\ell_{\infty}
-error contraction.
Suppose Assumptions
3
,
4
,
B.7
, and
B.8
hold with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
. If
(\bm{U}^{0},\bm{V}^{0})
satisfies (
13
) and

{\|\bm{U}^{0}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{U}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}\vee\|\bm{V}^{0}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{V}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}}\leq\;\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{nq}{\omega_{*}}\;\text{ with }\;\psi_{nq}\leq\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\;,\;\tfrac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa^{3/2}\sqrt{r}\tfrac{\phi_{nq}}{\psi_{nq}}\leq c_{0},

(15)

for some sequence
\psi_{nq}
and some sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
, then for all
t\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, (
14
) continues to hold, and in addition,

dist_{\infty}\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\}\;\leq\;\rho^{t}\psi_{nq}\omega_{*}.

(16)

Theorem
2
establishes the linear convergence of gradient descent under the asymmetric model. Compared with Theorem
1
for the symmetric model, the initialization requirement is slightly stronger, as the analysis must additionally ensure that
\bm{\Lambda}_{t}^{*}-\bm{I}_{r}
is small along the entire trajectory. Our requirement on
\kappa
aligns with the results in the matrix sensing literature
[
33
]
. At the same time, our setting is more general, as it allows for a broad class of loss functions
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
, for which the local curvature parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
may differ in order.

3
Optimization under Noise

We now consider the estimation problem under a stochastic objective
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
, starting again from the symmetric model. Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space carrying the randomness in the data or noise. For each
\omega\in\Omega
, the loss is redefined as
\mathcal{L}(\cdot\,;\,\omega):\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z};\omega):=\nabla_{\bm{X}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal};\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with
\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z}):=\mathbb{E}\,\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z};\omega)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z};\omega):=\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z};\omega)-\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z})
,
where the expectation is taken with respect to
\mathbb{P}
. The true parameter satisfies
\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z}^{*})={\bm{0}}
. When the dependence on
\omega
is not essential, we write
\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})
,
\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z})
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z})
in place of
\mathcal{L}(\bm{X};\omega)
,
\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z};\omega)
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z};\omega)
.

We introduce the following quantities to quantify the noise. For problem size
n
, tolerance parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, and local region
\mathcal{D}
, let
\Delta_{2}(n,\delta)
,
\Delta_{\infty}(n,\delta)
, and
\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,\delta)
be deterministic quantities such that, with probability at least
1\!-\!\delta
, the following hold for either
\mathcal{D}\!=\!\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
:

\displaystyle{\sup}_{\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}}\;n^{-1}{\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z})\|}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\quad\Delta_{2}(n,\delta),

(17a)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z}^{*})\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}}{\sqrt{n}\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|}\;\leq\;\Delta_{\infty}(n,\delta),

\displaystyle\,\quad{\sup}_{\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}}\;\tfrac{\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z})\|_{\infty\to 1}}{n}\;\leq\;{\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,\delta)}.

(17b)

Here,
\|\bm{M}\|_{\infty\to 1}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\sum_{j=1}^{q}|M_{ij}|
denotes maximum
\ell_{1}
row-sum of
\bm{M}=(m_{ij})_{n\times q}
.
The quantity
\Delta_{2}(n,\delta)
controls the overall noise in the operator norm, as enforced by (
17a
). In many linear problems
[
38
,
48
,
55
]
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z})
is independent of
\bm{Z}
and
\Delta_{2}(n,\delta)
can be readily derived by standard random matrix bounds
[
2
]
. For (
17b
),
\Delta_{\infty}(n,\delta)
controls the row-wise noise along the factor
\bm{Z}^{*}
. This quantity is intrinsic to
\ell_{\infty}
analysis by noting that
2\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z}^{*})\bm{Z}^{*}
equals the gradient noise at truth:
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}^{*})-\mathbb{E}\nabla_{\bm{Z}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}^{*})
.
For
\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,\delta)
in (
17b
), it captures worst-case
\ell_{\infty}
control and is used to bound terms such as
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z})(\bm{Z}^{t}-\bm{Z}^{*})\|_{2\to\infty}
. In general, without such structural information on the loss, the directions of
\bm{Z}^{t}-\bm{Z}^{*}
are difficult to characterize uniformly, which motivates imposing a worst-case bound in the general theory. For models with additional structure, this condition can often be relaxed with problem-specific arguments; see Example
2
. As a benchmark, consider the loss
\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})=\|\bm{X}-\bm{X}^{*}+\bm{E}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}/(2n)
, where the entries of
\bm{E}
are independent mean-zero sub-Gaussian random variables with sub-Gaussian norm
\sigma
, and as proved in Appendix
E.1
, one may take

\Delta_{2}(n,\delta)\asymp\sigma\tfrac{\sqrt{n+\log(1/\delta)}}{n};\quad\Delta_{\infty}(n,\delta)\asymp\sigma\sqrt{\tfrac{r+\log(n/\delta)}{n}};\quad\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,\delta)\asymp\sigma+\sigma\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(n/\delta)}{n}}.

(18)

Next, we present the convergence results for the
symmetric model
under statistical noise.

Theorem 3

(
Symmetric and Noisy).

Let
\bm{X}^{*}=\bm{Z}^{*}(\bm{Z}^{*})^{\intercal}
,
\sigma_{\min}=\sigma_{r}(\bm{X}^{*})/n
and
\kappa=\sigma_{1}(\bm{X}^{*})/\sigma_{r}(\bm{X}^{*})
. Let the iterates
\{\bm{Z}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be generated by (
3
) with step size
\eta^{t}=\eta=\{10(\alpha+\beta)\kappa\sigma_{\min}\}^{-1}
and
\rho=1-\eta\alpha\sigma_{\min}/4
. Assume the local radius is taken to satisfy
\epsilon\leq c_{0}{\alpha}/{(\kappa\sqrt{r})}
for some sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
.

\ell_{2}
-error contraction.
Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
and
\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z})
in place of
\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z})
. Suppose (
17a
) holds with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
, and assume for a sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
,

{\Delta_{2}(n,\delta)}/{(\alpha\sigma_{\min})}\leq c_{0}\epsilon.

Suppose the initialization
\bm{Z}^{0}
satisfies (
8
). Then, for some constant
C
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

dist_{2}(\bm{Z}^{t},\bm{Z}^{*})\;\leq\;\big\{\rho^{t}\phi_{n}+C\tfrac{\Delta_{2}(n,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\big\}\ \|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

(19)

\ell_{\infty}
-error contraction.
Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
,
B.5
, and
B.6
hold with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
and
\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{Z})
in place of
\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z})
. Suppose (
17
) holds with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
, where

\big(\tfrac{\beta}{\alpha}+\sqrt{r}\big)\kappa\sqrt{r}\tfrac{\Delta_{\infty}(n,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\leq c_{0}\epsilon,\quad\big(\tfrac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa\sqrt{\kappa r}+r\kappa\big)\tfrac{\Delta_{2}(n,\delta)}{\Delta_{\infty}(n,\delta)}\leq c_{0},\quad\tfrac{\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\leq\tfrac{1}{4},

for a sufficiently small constant
c_{0}>0
. Suppose
\bm{Z}^{0}
satisfies (
8
) and (
10
). Then, for some constant
C
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, (
19
) continues to hold and

dist_{\infty}(\bm{Z}^{t},\bm{Z}^{*})\;\leq\;\big\{\rho^{t}\psi_{n}+C\tfrac{\Delta_{\infty}(n,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\big\}\ \|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}.

(20)

Both the
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
errors comprise a linearly decaying term and a non-decaying term. The former is exactly the algorithmic error already seen in the noiseless setting (Theorem
1
), while the latter captures the statistical error induced by noise. Specifically, if
\widehat{\bm{Z}}
is some local minimizer of
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
, Appendix
C.3
shows that, for some constant
C
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

{dist_{2}(\widehat{\bm{Z}},\bm{Z}^{*})}\leq C\tfrac{\Delta_{2}(n,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\quad\text{and}\quad{dist_{\infty}(\widehat{\bm{Z}},\bm{Z}^{*})}\leq C\tfrac{\Delta_{\infty}(n,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\|\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}.

A key step in the proof is to use
\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}}
as the contraction target for
\widehat{\bm{R}}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{R}\in\mathcal{O}^{r}}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}\bm{R}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
. We show that
\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}}
is uniquely defined whenever
\widehat{\bm{Z}}
lies in the local region and satisfies the first-order condition
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\intercal})={\bm{0}}
. This is a highly nontrivial result because
\widehat{\bm{Z}}
is obtained from a constrained local minimization over
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
. It provides a valid local target around which the iterates contract in the noisy setting. The proof of Theorem
3
then follows by adapting the argument of Theorem
1
to this new target. Thus, although Theorem
1
can be viewed as a special case of Theorem
3
, we present it separately to isolate the optimization aspect of the result. It also serves as the conceptual and technical foundation for the noisy analysis.

We next turn to the
asymmetric model
under statistical noise. We adopt the same stochastic notation as in the symmetric case, with the loss now given by
\mathcal{L}(\cdot\,;\,\omega):\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}\to\mathbb{R}
. The first-order derivative is
\mathcal{G}(\bm{U},\bm{V};\omega):=\nabla_{\bm{X}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal};\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
, with
\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U},\bm{V})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U},\bm{V};\omega)
denoting the corresponding mean and perturbation terms. The true parameter now satisfies
\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})={\bm{0}}
. As before, we suppress the dependence on
\omega
when it is not essential. For a given problem size
(n,q)
, tolerance parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, and local region
\mathcal{D}
, let
\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)
,
\Delta_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)
, and
\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)
be deterministic quantities such that, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the following hold:

\displaystyle{\sup}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})\in\mathcal{D}}\;(nq)^{-1/2}{\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U},\bm{V})\|}\leq\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta),

(21a)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\bm{V}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}}{\sqrt{q}\|\bm{V}^{*}\|}\vee\tfrac{\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{U}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}}{\sqrt{n}\|\bm{U}^{*}\|}\leq\Delta_{\infty}(n,q,\delta),

(21b)

\displaystyle{\sup}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})\in\mathcal{D}}\;q^{-1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U},\bm{V})\|_{\infty\to 1}\vee n^{-1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U},\bm{V})^{\intercal}\|_{\infty\to 1}\leq\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,q,\delta).

(21c)

The convergence results for the asymmetric model with statistical noise are stated as follows.

Theorem 4

(
Asymmetric and Noisy).

Let
\bm{X}^{*}=\bm{U}^{*}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal}
and
\kappa=\sigma_{1}(\bm{X}^{*})/\sigma_{r}(\bm{X}^{*})
. Assume that
(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})
are balanced:

n^{-1}(\bm{U}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{U}^{*}=q^{-1}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{V}^{*},\;\text{ with }\;\sigma_{\min}:={\sigma_{r}(\bm{X}^{*})}/{\sqrt{nq}}={\sigma_{r}(\bm{U}^{*})^{2}}/{n}={\sigma_{r}(\bm{V}^{*})^{2}}/{q}.

Let
\{\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be generated by (
2
) with
\eta^{t}=\eta=\{10(\alpha+\beta)\kappa\sigma_{\min}\}^{-1}
and
\rho=1-\eta\alpha\sigma_{\min}/4
. Assume the local radius is taken to satisfy
\epsilon\leq c_{0}{\alpha}/{(\kappa\sqrt{r})}
for some sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
.

\ell_{2}
-error contraction.
Suppose Assumptions
3
,
4
hold with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
and
\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U},\bm{V})
in place of
\mathcal{G}(\bm{U},\bm{V})
. Suppose (
21a
) holds with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
, where the following holds

{\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)}/{(\alpha\sigma_{\min})}\leq c_{0}\epsilon,

for a sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
. Suppose initialization
(\bm{U}^{0},\bm{V}^{0})
satisfies (
13
).
Then, for some constant
C
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

dist_{2}\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\}\;\leq\;\big\{\rho^{t}\phi_{nq}+C\tfrac{\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\big\}\,\tau_{*}.

(22)

\ell_{\infty}
-error contraction.
Suppose Assumptions
3
,
4
,
B.7
, and
B.8
hold with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
and
\bar{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U},\bm{V})
in place of
\mathcal{G}(\bm{U},\bm{V})
. Suppose (
21
) holds with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
, where

\big(\tfrac{\beta}{\alpha}+\sqrt{r}\big)\kappa\sqrt{r}\tfrac{\Delta_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\leq c_{0}\epsilon,\quad\big(\tfrac{\beta}{\alpha}\kappa\sqrt{\kappa r}+{r\kappa}\big)\tfrac{\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)}{\Delta_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)}\leq c_{0},\quad\tfrac{\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\leq\tfrac{1}{4},

(23)

for a sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
. Suppose
(\bm{U}^{0},\bm{V}^{0})
satisfies (
13
) and (
15
). Then, for some constant
C
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, (
22
) continues to hold and we further have

dist_{\infty}\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\}\;\leq\;\big\{\rho^{t}\psi_{nq}+C\tfrac{\Delta_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}\big\}\,\omega_{*}.

(24)

To the best of our knowledge, Theorem
4
provides the first convergence guarantee for regularization-free gradient descent in noisy asymmetric low-rank estimation, a setting where even model-specific results were previously unavailable. It completes our framework, which covers symmetric and asymmetric models, both with and without noise, and establishes contractions in both
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
errors. It reveals a common mechanism across these settings: the nonconvex procedure admits a disguised convexity. The regularity conditions, initialization requirements, and noise bounds can then be verified in a model-specific manner to certify this mechanism in each problem.

We close this section with two concrete examples. Due to space limit, we summarize the informal results below and defer the formal ones to Appendix
A
.

Example 1
.

Matrix sensing is a central problem in information processing and machine learning, where the goal is to recover a low-rank matrix
\bm{X}^{*}
from a small number of linear measurements
[
26
,
39
,
45
]
. Consider the model

y_{i}=\langle\bm{A}_{i},\bm{X}^{*}\rangle+\xi_{i}\,\text{ for }i\in[m],

where
\{\bm{A}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
are sensing matrices known a priori,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the Frobenius inner product, and
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
are i.i.d. mean-zero sub-Gaussian random variables with sub-Gaussian norm
\sigma_{\xi}
.
Apply the gradient descent (
2
) to the loss
\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\langle\bm{A}_{i},\bm{X}\rangle-y_{i})^{2}/2m
.
Under suitable regularity conditions, we can apply Theorem
4
to obtain the
\ell_{2}
error bound as in (
22
) with probability at least
1-\delta
, with
\alpha
,
\beta\asymp 1
and

\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)\;\asymp\;\sigma_{\xi}\left(\tfrac{\log\{(n+q)/\delta\}}{m(n\wedge q)}\right)^{1/2}.

A related regularization-free convergence guarantee for gradient descent was established by
33
, but only in the noiseless setting, i.e.,
\xi_{i}=0
. Our initialization requirement matches theirs; see Theorem
A.5
in Appendix
A
and the subsequent discussion.

Example 2
.

Consider a Bernoulli low-rank response model with independent observations

Y_{ij}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(P_{ij}^{*}),\quad P_{ij}^{*}=\{1+\exp(-\alpha_{0}-X_{ij}^{*})\}^{-1}\quad\bm{X}^{*}=(X_{ij}^{*})_{n\times q}=\bm{U}^{*}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal},

for
i\in[n]
and
j\in[q]
,
where
\alpha_{0}
is a known intercept and
\bm{X}^{*}
is a rank-
r
signal matrix.
The intercept
\alpha_{0}
accommodates sparse binary data, since a negative
\alpha_{0}
can make all success probabilities small even when
\bm{X}^{*}
is low-rank. Such models arise in generalized low-rank models
[
46
,
18
]
, network representation learning
[
30
,
35
]
, and binary matrix completion
[
5
,
21
]
.
Assume that, for some
M_{1},M_{2}
,
-M_{1}\leq X_{ij}^{*}\leq M_{2},
for
i\in[n],\ j\in[q]
.
Apply the gradient descent scheme (
2
) to the scaled loss
\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\nu_{\star}\ell(X_{ij}+\alpha_{0};Y_{ij})
with
\ell(x,y)=\log(1+\exp(x))-yx
and
\nu_{\star}:=e^{-(\alpha_{0}+M_{2})}
. Then under suitable regularity conditions, the
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
error bounds in Theorem
4
hold with probability at least
1-\delta
with
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-(M_{1}+M_{2})}
,
\beta=1
,

\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)\asymp\big(\tfrac{\nu_{\star}}{n\wedge q}+\tfrac{\nu_{\star}^{2}\log((n+q)/\delta)}{nq}\big)^{1/2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Delta_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)\asymp\big(\tfrac{r+\log((n\vee q)/\delta)}{n\wedge q}\ \nu_{\star}R_{\star}\big)^{1/2},

for some properly selected
R_{\star}
.
This result demonstrates the applicability of our theory to nonlinear models, which are substantially more challenging than linear settings.
The closest existing result studies a projected gradient descent algorithm and provides only
\ell_{2}
guarantees
[
35
]
, while our theory also establishes the
\ell_{\infty}
result. Moreover, our theory covers the near-optimal sparsity regime where
e^{\alpha_{0}}\asymp(n\wedge q)^{-1+\varepsilon}
for any fixed
\varepsilon
, where regularization-free trajectory guarantees of this type were previously unavailable for such models.

It is worth noting that for this model, we adopt a leave-one-out argument to avoid the condition on
\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)
. This provides a precise characterization of the dependence between iterates
(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})
and the statistical noise, while it also requires certain technical regularity conditions on the leave-one-out initializers; see Theorem
A.6
in Appendix
A
and the subsequent discussion. The formal guarantee is hence conditional on these leave-one-out initialization conditions and holds uniformly over
0\leq t\leq T_{\star}
. Here,
T_{\star}
denotes the maximum number of iterations, which enters the scaling conditions through logarithmic factors, due to a union bound over the trajectory. In the considered regime, taking
T_{\star}\asymp\log(n\vee q)
is sufficient for the linearly decaying term to match the statistical error. This example shows that, with additional model structure, the general condition in Theorems
3
and
4
on
\bar{\Delta}_{\infty}(\cdot)
can be relaxed.

4
Conclusion

This paper develops a theoretical framework for nonconvex procedures in low-rank estimation. Our key device is an equivalent formulation of the original nonconvex procedure into a strongly convex one within a local region, achieved via a benign regularizer that does not alter the update. This benign regularizer makes explicit the implicit structural feature that governs the mechanism of the nonconvex algorithm. Our framework offers a new geometric explanation for the stable behavior of the regularization-free nonconvex procedure and lays a foundation for analyzing more complex models in which a low-rank matrix is only one component, such as low-rank-plus-sparse models
[
6
]
, LoRA fine-tuning
[
25
]
, and deep matrix factorization
[
1
]
.

Contents

Appendix A
Example Details

A.1
Matrix Sensing

We provide the details for Example
1
. Recall that the observations follow

y_{i}=\langle\bm{A}_{i},\bm{X}^{*}\rangle+\xi_{i},\qquad i\in[m],

where
\bm{X}^{*}=\bm{U}^{*}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
has rank
r
. Here,
\langle\bm{A},\bm{B}\rangle={\rm Tr}(\bm{A}\bm{B}^{\intercal})
denotes the Frobenius inner product where
{\rm Tr}(\cdot)
is the trace operator. Throughout, we condition our analysis on the sensing matrices
\{\bm{A}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
, so that the probability statements are taken with respect to the measurement noise
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
.
Define the sensing operator
\mathcal{A}(\bm{X}):=\big(\langle\bm{A}_{1},\bm{X}\rangle,\dots,\langle\bm{A}_{m},\bm{X}\rangle\big)
. Assume that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the rank-
2r
restricted isometry property: for any rank-
2r
matrix
\bm{X}
, for some
0<\delta_{0}<1
such that

(1-\delta_{0})\|\bm{X}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq m^{-1}\|\mathcal{A}(\bm{X})\|^{2}\leq(1+\delta_{0})\|\bm{X}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

(A.1)

This condition is standard in the matrix sensing literature
[
26
,
33
,
39
,
45
]
, and holds for a broad class of measurement ensembles
[
39
]
. The loss is
\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})=(2m)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(\langle\bm{A}_{i},\bm{X}\rangle-y_{i}\big)^{2}
. We then apply the first part of Theorem
4
to obtain

Theorem A.5
.

Assume that
(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})
are balanced as in Theorem
4
and the sample size satisfies

m\gg\frac{(1+\delta_{0})\kappa^{2}r\sigma_{\xi}^{2}}{(1-\delta_{0})^{4}\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\log\{(n\vee q)/\delta\}}{n\wedge q},

If the initialization
(\bm{U}^{0},\bm{V}^{0})
satisfies (
13
) for
\epsilon=\infty
, for the iterates
\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})\}_{t\geq 0}
generated by (
2
) with step size
\eta^{t}=\eta=(20\kappa\sigma_{\min})^{-1}
, we have with probability at least
1-\delta
,

dist_{2}\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\}\leq\left(\;\rho^{t}\phi_{nq}+C\frac{\sigma_{\xi}}{(1-\delta_{0})\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{(1+\delta_{0})\log((n+q)/\delta)}{m(n\wedge q)}}\;\right)\tau_{*},

where
\rho=1-(1-\delta_{0})/(80\kappa)
.

Proof.

See Section
E.2
. Here, we take
\alpha=1-\delta_{0}
,
\beta=1+\delta_{0}
, and

\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)\asymp\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\tfrac{(1+\delta_{0})\log((n+q)/\delta)}{m(n\wedge q)}}.

The result in Example
1
is obtained by taking
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
to be some absolute constant.
∎

Our result is closely related to the balancing-free analysis of
33
for asymmetric low-rank matrix sensing. They consider the noiseless model and show that, under a rank-
2r
RIP condition, regularization-free gradient descent converges linearly once the initialization lies in a sufficiently small balanced basin of attraction. In our notation, their initialization condition can be written as

\phi_{nq}\lesssim\frac{c_{0}\sqrt{\sigma_{\min}}}{\tau_{*}\kappa^{3/2}}=\frac{c_{0}}{\kappa^{5/2}\sqrt{r}},

which matches ours up to constants when
\delta_{0}
is bounded away from one. For the sample size assumption, it arises only in the noisy setting. When
\xi_{i}=0
, we can apply Theorem
2
directly to obtain the convergence guarantee

dist_{2}\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\}\leq\rho^{t}\phi_{nq}\tau_{*},

provided (
A.1
) holds. In this case, no sample size condition is needed, which recovers the result of
33
as a special case.
Moreover, we note that our sample size requirement is also close to the information-theoretical optimum where
m\asymp r(n\vee q)
up to logarithmic factors
[
8
,
39
]
.

Initialization can be obtained using a similar strategy presented in
33
. One may first construct a rank-
r
spectral or projected-gradient estimator of
\bm{X}^{*}
, and then take its balanced factorization as
(\bm{U}^{0},\bm{V}^{0})
. It can be verified that these approaches can satisfy our initialization requirement as long as the perturbation from
m^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\xi_{i}\bm{A}_{i}
is sufficiently small.

A.2
Low-rank Bernoulli response model

We now describe the setup for the low-rank Bernoulli response model in detail. Recall that
Y_{ij}\sim{\rm Bernoulli}(P_{ij}^{*})
for
P_{ij}^{*}=1/\{1+e^{-(\alpha_{0}+X^{*}_{ij})}\}
. Let

\sigma(x):=\sigma_{0}(\alpha_{0}+x)=\frac{\exp(\alpha_{0}+x)}{1+\exp(\alpha_{0}+x)}.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\alpha_{0}\leq 0
, since one can always flip the labels
0
and
1
so that
0
becomes the majority observation. We use
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
to quantify the range of the linear predictors, and assume without loss of generality that

-M_{1}\leq(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal})_{i,j}\leq M_{2},\qquad\forall\,i\in[n],\ j\in[q],

for any
(\bm{U},\bm{V})\in\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
. Here, subscript
\bm{A}_{i,j}
denotes the
(i,j)
th entry of matrix
\bm{A}
. Since
\epsilon
can be taken sufficiently small in the subsequent analysis, this condition can always be ensured by enlarging
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
slightly by constant factors. Let
M_{\star}=M_{1}+M_{2}
.

For the scaled loss function
\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\nu_{\star}\ell(X_{ij};Y_{ij})
for
\ell(x;y)=\log\{1+\exp(\alpha_{0}+x)\}-yx
and
\nu_{\star}=e^{-(\alpha_{0}+M_{2})}
, under the notation of Assumption
3
, one can verify that
\alpha
and
\beta
may be chosen as

\alpha=\frac{1}{4}e^{-M_{\star}},\qquad\beta=1.

Now we state our result.

Theorem A.6
.

Assume that
(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})
are balanced as in Theorem
4
. Fix a maximal iteration count for (
2
) as
T_{\star}
. Let
\zeta_{r}:=\big(\tfrac{\beta}{\alpha}+\sqrt{r}\big)\sqrt{r}\,\kappa
,
\ell_{\star}=\log\{(n+q)T_{\star}/\delta\}
,
L_{\star}=r+\ell_{\star}
. We assume that for any small constant
c_{0}
, the following holds

\displaystyle\frac{(\nu_{\star}+1)L_{\star}}{n\wedge q}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}

(A.2)

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\left\{\left(\alpha^{-1}\kappa\sqrt{\kappa r}+\kappa r\right)\zeta_{r}\right\}^{2}}{L_{\star}}+\frac{\nu_{\star}\kappa L_{\star}(\omega_{*}^{4}+\omega_{*}^{-2})}{(n\wedge q)\alpha^{4}\sigma_{\min}^{4}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\frac{\alpha^{2}\sigma_{\min}^{2}\epsilon^{2}(n\wedge q)}{\nu_{\star}L_{\star}\kappa^{2}r(\alpha^{-1}+\sqrt{r})^{2}}.

Then we can select
R_{\star}
such that for sufficiently large constant
C
, it holds that

R_{\star}=C\left[\frac{\left\{\left(\alpha^{-1}\kappa\sqrt{\kappa r}+\kappa r\right)\zeta_{r}\right\}^{2}}{L_{\star}}+\frac{\nu_{\star}\kappa L_{\star}(\omega_{*}^{4}+\omega_{*}^{-2})}{(n\wedge q)\alpha^{4}\sigma_{\min}^{4}}\right]

(A.3)

and we set

\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)\asymp\sqrt{\frac{\nu_{\star}}{n\wedge q}+\frac{\nu_{\star}^{2}\ell_{\star}}{nq}},\quad\Delta_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)\asymp\sqrt{\frac{\nu_{\star}L_{\star}R_{\star}}{n\wedge q}}.

(A.4)

Assume that the initialization
(\bm{U}^{0},\bm{V}^{0})
satisfies
(
13
) and (
15
) with
\epsilon\leq c_{0}\alpha/(\kappa\sqrt{r})
for sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
, and that the following leave-one-out initializers are available.

1.

For each
i\in[n]
, there is a row-wise LOO initializer
(\bm{U}^{0,-i},\bm{V}^{0,-i})
, measurable to
\sigma\big(\bm{Y}^{-i,}\big)
.

2.

For each
\ell\in[q]
, there is a column-wise LOO initializer
(\bm{U}^{0,-\ell},\bm{V}^{0,-\ell})
, measurable to
\sigma\big(\bm{Y}^{,-\ell}\big)
.

Here,
(\bm{Y}^{-i})_{k\ell}:=Y_{k\ell}1_{\{k\neq i\}}+P^{*}_{k\ell}1_{\{k=i\}},\text{ for }i,k\in[n],\ \ell\in[q]
, and
(\bm{Y}^{-\ell})_{kj}:=Y_{kj}1_{\{j\neq\ell\}}+P^{*}_{kj}1_{\{j=\ell\}},\text{ for }k\in[n],\ \ell,j\in[q]
.
Let
\phi_{nq}^{\dagger}:=\phi_{nq}+C\tfrac{\Delta_{2}(n,q,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}
and
\psi_{nq}^{\dagger}:=\psi_{nq}+C\tfrac{\Delta_{\infty}(n,q,\delta)}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}
.
Assume further that, for the same orthogonal matrix
\bm{R}^{0}
in (
13
) and (
15
), these LOO initializers satisfy

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n]}\frac{\big\|\bm{U}^{0,-i,}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{U}^{0}\bm{R}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\sqrt{n}}\vee\frac{\big\|\bm{V}^{0,-i}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{V}^{0}\bm{R}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\sqrt{q}}\leq c_{0}\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}{\sqrt{\nu_{\star}\beta+\nu_{\star}^{2}L_{\star}/q}}\,\psi_{nq}^{\dagger}\omega_{*};

\displaystyle\max_{\ell\in[q]}\frac{\big\|\bm{U}^{0,-\ell}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{U}^{0}\bm{R}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\sqrt{n}}\vee\frac{\big\|\bm{V}^{0,-\ell}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{V}^{0}\bm{R}^{0}\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\sqrt{q}}\leq\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\min}}{\sqrt{\nu_{\star}\beta+\nu_{\star}^{2}L_{\star}/n}}\,\psi_{nq}^{\dagger}\omega_{*};

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n]}\left\{\|\bm{U}^{0,-i}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{U}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}\vee\|\bm{V}^{0,-i}(\bm{R}^{0})^{-{\intercal}}-\bm{V}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}\right\}\leq c_{0}\psi_{nq}^{\dagger}\omega_{*};

\displaystyle\max_{\ell\in[q]}\left\{\|\bm{U}^{0,-\ell}\bm{R}^{0}-\bm{U}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}\vee\|\bm{V}^{0,-\ell}(\bm{R}^{0})^{-{\intercal}}-\bm{V}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}\right\}\leq c_{0}\psi_{nq}^{\dagger}\omega_{*},

for sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
.
Let the iterates
\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})\}_{t\geq 0}
be generated by (
2
)
with step size

\eta^{t}=\eta=\{10(\alpha+\beta)\kappa\sigma_{\min}\}^{-1},\qquad\rho:=1-\eta\alpha\sigma_{\min}/4.

(A.5)

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
error contractions of
Theorem
4
hold for the Bernoulli model uniformly for all iterations
0\leq t\leq T_{\star}
:

\displaystyle dist_{2}\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\}\leq\rho^{t}\phi_{nq}\tau_{*}+C\Big\{\frac{\nu_{\star}}{n\wedge q}+\frac{\nu_{\star}^{2}\log((n+q)/\delta)}{nq}\Big\}^{1/2}\,\frac{\tau_{*}}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}};

\displaystyle dist_{\infty}\{(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t}),(\bm{U}^{*},\bm{V}^{*})\}\leq\rho^{t}\psi_{nq}\omega_{*}+C\Big\{\frac{r+\log((n\vee q)/\delta)}{n\wedge q}\ R_{\star}\Big\}^{1/2}\,\frac{\omega_{*}}{\alpha\sigma_{\min}},

Proof.

See Section
E.3
.
∎

The scaling conditions in (
A.2
) are mild and imply that the theory guarantees convergence in the near-optimal sparse regime. Suppose, for simplicity, that
r,\kappa,\sigma_{\min},\omega_{*},M_{1},M_{2}
are constants and that
T_{\star}\asymp\log(n\vee q)
. Then
L_{\star}=r+\log\{(n\vee q)T_{\star}/\delta\}\asymp\log\{(n\vee q)/\delta\}
up to a logarithmic factor. Then conditions in (
A.2
) reduce to
\tfrac{\nu_{\star}L_{\star}}{n\wedge q}\ll 1
. Equivalently,

e^{\alpha_{0}}(n\wedge q)\gg\log\{(n\vee q)/\delta\}.

This condition allows
e^{\alpha_{0}}\asymp(n\wedge q)^{-1+\varepsilon}
, for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
, which matches the information-theoretically near-optimal scaling
nqe^{\alpha_{0}}\asymp(n\vee q)
up to logarithmic factors.
To the best of our knowledge, this is the first convergence guarantee for such models in the optimal sparsity regime.

The initialization assumptions in Theorem
A.6
should be viewed as technical conditions needed for the leave-one-out argument. They are imposed so that one can construct LOO gradient descent sequences that track the trajectory of
(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})
while enjoying favorable dependence structures (see details in Lemma
14
). In particular, these auxiliary sequences remain close to
(\bm{U}^{t},\bm{V}^{t})
but are independent of certain rows or columns of
\bm{Y}
. This reflects an underlying geometric property in the optimization problem, where each row of
\bm{U}^{t}
and
\bm{V}^{t}
is not significantly affected by any single row or column of
\bm{Y}
. In other words, near the optimization trajectory, the statistical noise
\bm{Y}-\bm{P}^{*}
influences the landscape in an approximately uniform way. As for constructing initializers satisfying the stated rates, this is beyond the scope of this paper, and we leave this question for future work.

The quantity
T_{\star}
appears only through logarithmic factors. It is included because the LOO sequences allow us to apply a Bernstein inequality to terms of the form
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(\bm{U}^{-i,t},\bm{V}^{-i,t})\Delta
at each step, where the relevant difference
\Delta
is independent of the row or column noise being averaged.
These high-probability events are required to hold uniformly over all iterations
0\leq t\leq T_{\star}
, and the proof takes a union bound over these iterations.

Appendix B
Additional Assumptions

We introduce additional technical assumptions needed to establish
\ell_{\infty}
convergence. Denote
\bm{e}_{i}^{(n)}
the canonical basis in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For any matrix
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
and
i\in[n]
,
\bm{M}_{i,\cdot}
denotes its
i
th row. The following is a row-wise control on the Hessian matrix.

Assumption B.5
.

There exists some
\epsilon
and
\beta>0
such that for either
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
, the following holds: for any
\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}
,
i\in[n]
,
\bm{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
, and
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
satisfying
\bm{W}_{i,\cdot}=0
, by letting
\bm{W}_{i}=\bm{e}_{i}^{(n)}\bm{h}^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
, it holds that

\big|\nabla_{\bm{X}}^{2}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})\big[\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W}_{i}),\,\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W})\big]\big|\leq\beta\,\big(\|\bm{Z}_{i,\cdot}\|\|\bm{h}\|\big)\,\big(\|\bm{Z}\|\|\bm{W}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\big),

where
\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W}):=\bm{Z}\bm{W}^{\intercal}+\bm{W}\bm{Z}^{\intercal}
.

Assumption
B.5
strengthens the local RIP condition by adding a row-wise control on the Hessian. Here,
\beta
may differ from the one in Assumption
1
; for simplicity, we use the same notation, since one may take
\beta
to be the maximum of the two constants.
While Assumption
1
controls the overall size of the curvature on tangent directions, it does not rule out anisotropic behavior in which perturbation concentrating on one row interacts strongly with perturbations supported away from that row. A proper row-wise control is important for the
\ell_{\infty}
-analysis. The assumption is mild for loss function
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
whose Hessian structure is diagonal, or approximately diagonal. For example, if

\nabla_{\bm{X}}^{2}\mathcal{L}(\bm{X})[\bm{H}_{1},\bm{H}_{2}]=\sum_{a,b}\omega_{ab}(X)\,\bm{H}_{1,ab}\bm{H}_{2,ab},\qquad|\omega_{ab}(\bm{X})|\leq\beta,

then
\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{e}_{i}\bm{h}^{\intercal})
only overlaps with the
i
th row and column of
\mathcal{P}_{Z}(\bm{W})
, which gives

\big|\nabla_{\bm{X}}^{2}\mathcal{L}(X)[\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{e}_{i}^{(n)}\bm{h}^{\intercal}),\mathcal{P}_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{W})]\big|\leq C\beta\|\bm{Z}_{i,\cdot}\|\|\bm{Z}\|\|\bm{h}\|\,\|\bm{W}\|_{\mathrm{F}},\qquad\bm{W}_{i,\cdot}=0.

Hence Assumption
B.5
holds naturally in entrywise models such as Example
2
. Geometrically, the RIP controls the strength of curvature, whereas Assumption
B.5
controls how localized that curvature remains in the ambient coordinates.

Assumption
B.6
is the additional requirement for deriving
\ell_{\infty}
error contraction.

Assumption B.6
.

There exist some
\epsilon
and a constant
L_{\infty}
such that for all
\bm{Z}_{1},\bm{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
,
\|\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}_{1})-\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}_{2})\|_{2\to\infty}\leq L_{\infty}\|\bm{Z}_{1}\bm{Z}_{1}^{\intercal}-\bm{Z}_{2}\bm{Z}_{2}^{\intercal}\|_{2\to\infty}
, where
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
.

Similar to the above discussion, Assumption
B.6
is needed because a spectral norm bound on

\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}_{1})-\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z}_{2})
does not suffice to control the maximum row-wise size of the gradient perturbation. For entrywise loss functions, Assumption
B.6
typically follows from a uniform bound on the second-order derivative of the link function over the local region
\mathcal{D}
.

Next, we present two assumptions required for establishing
\ell_{\infty}
error contraction under the asymmetric model analogous to Assumptions
B.5
and
B.6
.

Assumption B.7
.

There exists some
\epsilon
and
\beta>0
such that, for either
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
, the following holds: for
(\bm{U},\bm{V})\in\mathcal{D}
,
i\in[n]
,
\bm{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
, by letting
\bm{L}_{i}=\bm{e}_{i}^{(n)}\bm{h}^{\intercal}
and
\bm{R}_{j}=\bm{e}_{j}^{(q)}\bm{h}^{\intercal}
, for every
(\bm{L},\bm{R})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}\times\mathbb{R}^{q\times r}
,

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{\bm{X}}^{2}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal})[\mathcal{P}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})}({\bm{0}},\bm{L}_{i}),\mathcal{P}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})}(\bm{R},\bm{L}_{-i})\big|\leq\beta\|\bm{V}\bm{h}\|\,\|\bm{U}_{i,}\bm{R}^{\intercal}\|,

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{\bm{X}}^{2}\mathcal{L}(\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal})[\mathcal{P}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})}(\bm{R}_{j},{\bm{0}}),\mathcal{P}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})}(\bm{R}_{-j},\bm{L})\big|\leq\beta\|\bm{U}\bm{h}\|\,\|\bm{L}(\bm{V}_{j,})^{\intercal}\|,

where
\mathcal{P}_{(\bm{U},\bm{V})}(\bm{R},\bm{L})=\bm{U}\bm{R}^{\intercal}+\bm{L}\bm{V}^{\intercal}
.
Here,
\bm{L}_{-i}
denotes the matrix obtained from
\bm{L}
by replacing its
i
th row with
{\bm{0}}
, and
\bm{R}_{-j}
is defined analogously by replacing the
j
th row of
\bm{R}
with
{\bm{0}}
.

Assumption B.8
.

There exist
\epsilon
and a constant
L_{\infty}>0
such that for all
(\bm{U}_{1},\bm{V}_{1}),(\bm{U}_{2},\bm{V}_{2})\in\mathcal{D}
,
\big\|\mathcal{G}(\bm{U}_{1},\bm{V}_{1})-\mathcal{G}(\bm{U}_{2},\bm{V}_{2})\big\|_{2\to\infty}\leq L_{\infty}\big\|\bm{U}_{1}\bm{V}_{1}^{\intercal}-\bm{U}_{2}\bm{V}_{2}^{\intercal}\big\|_{2\to\infty}
and
\big\|\{\mathcal{G}(\bm{U}_{1},\bm{V}_{1})-\mathcal{G}(\bm{U}_{2},\bm{V}_{2})\}^{\intercal}\big\|_{2\to\infty}\leq L_{\infty}\|\bm{V}_{1}\bm{U}_{1}^{\intercal}-\bm{V}_{2}\bm{U}_{2}^{\intercal}\|_{2\to\infty}
where
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
.

The assumptions introduced in this section are technical regularity conditions for row-wise control, imposed to establish
\ell_{\infty}
convergence, thereby enabling analysis under localizations
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
and
\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
. This analysis is important for two purposes. First,
\ell_{\infty}
convergence provides a sharper characterization of gradient descent trajectory than
\ell_{2}
control alone, and has played an important role in the analysis of nonconvex low-rank problems
[
12
,
15
,
34
]
. Second, the local RIP conditions in Assumptions
1
and
3
may fail under the larger localizations
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
and
\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
. For instance, in nonlinear models such as Example
2
, the curvature of the loss can vanish as the entries of
\bm{X}
diverge. Under
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
or
\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
, there is no guarantee that the largest entry of
\bm{X}
remains bounded. A row-wise localization of the factors is a natural way to control

\|\bm{U}\bm{V}^{\intercal}-\bm{U}^{*}(\bm{V}^{*})^{\intercal}\|_{\infty}

and thereby ensure nonvanishing local curvature. A similar case appears in matrix completion, where restricted curvature is available only over incoherent or entrywise-controlled low-rank matrices, rather than over the entire low-rank variety

[
14
,
27
,
41
,
55
]
. Finally, we note that in some settings, these row-wise assumptions may not hold without additional structure. For instance, in matrix sensing presented in Example
1
, standard analyses typically establish
\ell_{2}
convergence, rather than
\ell_{\infty}
one.
In such cases, since the RIP holds over the larger
\ell_{2}
localization
\mathcal{D}_{uv}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
, the first part of Theorem
4
still yields
\ell_{2}
convergence.
This suggests that the additional row-wise conditions are not merely proof artifacts, but may reflect structural requirements needed for
\ell_{\infty}
convergence.

Finally, we end this part with a summary of key notation used in the proof; see Table
1
.

Table 1:

Appendix C
Proofs of Results under Symmetric Model

This section proves the main results for the symmetric model in the deterministic setting (Theorem
1
) and the noisy setting (Theorem
3
). Section
C.1
introduces notation and technical lemmas used throughout this section. Sections
C.2
and
C.3
present proofs for
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
error contractions under localization
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
. We begin with this localization because it is the more delicate case, where the proof must control the
\ell_{2}
and
\ell_{\infty}
errors simultaneously along the entire trajectory. Section
C.4
then gives the
\ell_{2}
error contraction under the larger localization
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(2)}(\epsilon)
.

Before starting the formal proof, we provide a roadmap for the argument under localization
\mathcal{D}_{z}^{(\infty)}(\epsilon)
. The
noiseless
proof has three main components. First, the benign regularizer turns the aligned update into a gradient step on a locally strongly convex objective as in (
5
). This immediately yields the
\ell_{2}
error contraction. Second, using the same aligned update, we derive the
\ell_{\infty}
error for
\bm{Z}^{t+1}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}-\bm{Z}^{*}
. Assumptions
B.5
and
B.6
are used at this stage to control the row-wise effect of the second-order and first-order derivatives, respectively. Third, we quantify the rotation drift
\bm{R}_{t+1}^{*}-\bm{R}_{t}^{*}
and transfer the error bound for
\|\bm{Z}^{t+1}\bm{R}_{t}^{*}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}
to the optimally aligned error

dist_{\infty}(\bm{Z}^{t+1},\bm{Z}^{*})=\|\bm{Z}^{t+1}\bm{R}_{t+1}^{*}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{2\to\infty}.

In the
noisy
case, the iterates no longer contract exactly toward
\bm{Z}^{*}
. We therefore first analyze the optimizer
\widehat{\bm{Z}}
, which is a statistically perturbed version of
\bm{Z}^{*}
. Then with
\widehat{\bm{R}}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{R}\in\mathcal{O}^{r}}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}\bm{R}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
, we establish the first order condition for
\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}}
. Specifically, we show that
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\intercal})={\bm{0}}
and
\nabla_{\bm{Z}}p_{\alpha}^{*}(\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}})={\bm{0}}
. Thus
\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}}
is a valid contraction target for the noisy trajectory. We then re-center the analysis at
\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}}
and repeat the deterministic contraction argument, with the alignment now taken relative to this empirical target. The final error is the sum of the statistical error of
\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}}
and the algorithmic error towards
\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}}
.
We adopt this strategy because the non-decaying statistical error is absorbed into
\widehat{\bm{Z}}
, allowing the trajectory analysis to retain a purely contractive form up to the final statistical radius. For the same reason, although the noisy setting subsumes the noiseless one, we present our framework and results in the main text under the noiseless setting for clarity.

C.1
Preliminaries

We use
\{\bm{e}_{i}^{(n)}\}
to denote the canonical basis in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and we omit the superscript
n
when the dimension is clear from the context. For a vector
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a subset
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[n]
, write
\bm{x}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
for the sub-vector indexed by
{\mathcal{S}}
. For a matrix
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
and index sets
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\subseteq[n]
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\subseteq[m]
, write
\bm{M}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1},{\mathcal{S}}_{2}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\mathcal{S}}_{1}|\times|{\mathcal{S}}_{2}|}
for the corresponding submatrix. In particular,
\bm{M}_{i,\cdot}
and
\bm{M}_{\cdot,j}
denote the
i
th row and
j
th column, while
\bm{M}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1},\cdot}
and
\bm{M}_{\cdot,{\mathcal{S}}_{2}}
denote row- and column-restricted submatrices. Let
\otimes
be the Kronecker product.
Write
\bm{z}=\mathrm{vec}(\bm{Z}^{\intercal})
, so that
\nabla_{\bm{z}}^{2}
denotes the Hessian with respect to the vectorized variable; later,
\nabla_{\bm{z}_{i}\bm{z}_{j}}^{2}
denotes its
(i,j)
block.

Let

\bm{M}^{*}(\bm{Z})=n^{-1}\big\{(\bm{Z}^{*})^{\intercal}\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{\intercal}\bm{Z}^{*}\big\}.

Then the benign regularizer for the symmetric model can be written as
p_{\alpha}^{*}(\bm{Z})=\tfrac{\alpha n^{2}}{4}\|\bm{M}^{*}(\bm{Z})\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
. Without loss of generality, we assume
\alpha<1
. For matrix-valued maps such as
\bm{M}^{*}(\bm{Z})
, we write
D\bm{M}^{*}(\bm{Z})[\bm{W}]
for the Fréchet derivative at
\bm{Z}
applied to the direction
\bm{W}
, and
D^{2}\bm{M}^{*}(\bm{Z})[\bm{W}_{1},\bm{W}_{2}]
for the second Fréchet derivative.

For a scalar-valued function
f
and any direction
\bm{W}
,

\{\mathrm{vec}(\bm{W}^{\intercal})\}^{\intercal}\nabla_{\bm{z}}^{2}f(\bm{Z})\,\mathrm{vec}(\bm{W}^{\intercal})=\nabla_{\bm{Z}}^{2}f(\bm{Z})[\bm{W},\bm{W}].

Throughout the proof, all constants
C,c>0
are universal and may change from line to line. We typically use
c_{0}
as constants that can be sufficiently small. Let
\rho=1-\eta\alpha\sigma_{\min}/4
.

Finally, we introduce the following technical lemmas used in the subsequent proofs. We start with a slightly more general version of Lemma
2
:

Lemma 2
.

Under Assumption
1
, and with
\lambda=\alpha
, we have

\min_{\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}}\lambda_{\min}\big\{n^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{z}}^{2}h_{\alpha}^{*}(\bm{Z})-2n^{-1}\mathcal{G}(\bm{Z})\otimes\bm{I}_{r}\big\}\geq\alpha\sigma_{r}(n^{-1/2}\bm{Z}^{*})^{2}-4n^{-1}\max_{\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}}\|\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

Proof.

See Section
F.1
.
∎

We introduce the following result that bounds the eigenvalues for the principal submatrix of
\nabla_{\bm{z}}^{2}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})
.

Lemma 3
.

Under Assumption
1
,

\min_{\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}}\min_{i\in[n]}\lambda_{\min}\big\{n^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{\bm{z}_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})-2n^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{i,i}(\bm{Z})\bm{I}_{r}\big\}\geq\alpha\sigma_{r}(n^{-1/2}\bm{Z}^{*})^{2}-2n^{-1}\max_{\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}}\|\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

and

\max_{\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}}\max_{i\in[n]}\lambda_{\max}\big\{n^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{\bm{z}_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{\intercal})-2n^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{i,i}(\bm{Z})\bm{I}_{r}\big\}\leq\beta\sigma_{1}(n^{-1/2}\bm{Z}^{*})^{2}+2n^{-1}\max_{\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{D}}\|\bm{Z}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

Proof.

The proof follows from the same argument as in Lemma
2
by restricting the perturbation to the form
\bm{W}=\bm{e}_{i}\bm{a}^{\intercal}
for
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
. We omit the routine details.∎

This lemma presents the properties of the optimal alignment in the symmetric model.

Lemma 4

(Theorem 2 in
42
)
.

For any
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
,
\widehat{\bm{R}}^{*}
is the solution to
\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{R}\in\mathcal{O}^{r}}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}\bm{R}-\bm{Z}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
if and only if

\widehat{\bm{R}}^{*}{}^{\intercal}\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\intercal}\bm{Z}^{*}=(\bm{Z}^{*})^{\intercal}\widehat{\bm{Z}}\widehat{\bm{R}}^{*}\text{ is positive semidefinite}.

This lemma relates perturbation bounds in the product space
ZZ^{\intercal}
into aligned perturbation bounds for the factorization
\bm{Z}
itself.

Lemma 5

(Lemma 5.3 in
45
)
.

For any
\bm{Z}_{1},\bm{Z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
such that

dist_{2}(\bm{Z}_{1},\bm{Z}_{2})\leq c\|\bm{Z}_{1}\|
for some constant
c>0
, we have

\|\bm{Z}_{1}\bm{Z}_{1}^{\intercal}-\bm{Z}_{2}\bm{Z}_{2}^{\intercal}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq(2+c)\|\bm{Z}_{1}\|dist_{2}(\bm{Z}_{1},\bm{Z}_{2}).

This lemma controls the change of the optimal Procrustes alignment between
\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
when they are close in terms of Frobenius norm.

Lemma 6

(Theorem 2.3 in
36
)
.

Let
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
, and let
\bm{R}^{*}\in\mathcal{O}^{r}
be the optimal rotation defined by

\bm{R}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{R}\in\mathcal{O}^{r}}\|\bm{A}\bm{R}-\bm{B}\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

If
\sigma_{r}(\bm{B})>0
and
\|\bm{A}-\bm{B}\|_{\mathrm{F}}<\sigma_{r}(\bm{B})
